A General Upper Bound for the Runtime of a Coevolutionary Algorithm on Impartial Combinatorial Games
Authors: Alistair Benford, Per Kristian Lehre
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Due to their complex dynamics, combinatorial games are a key test case and application for algorithms that train game playing agents. Among those algorithms that train using self-play are coevolutionary algorithms (CoEAs). However, the successful application of CoEAs for game playing is difficult due to pathological behaviours such as cycling, an issue especially critical for games with intransitive payoff landscapes. Insight into how to design CoEAs to avoid such behaviours can be provided by runtime analysis. In this paper, we push the scope of runtime analysis for CoEAs to combinatorial games, proving a general upper bound for the number of simulated games needed for a simple estimation of distribution algorithm to discover (with high probability) an optimal strategy. This result applies to any impartial combinatorial game, and for many games the implied bound is polynomial or quasipolynomial as a function of the number of game positions. After proving the main result, we provide several applications to simple well-known games: Nim, Chomp, Silver Dollar, and Turning Turtles. As the first runtime analysis for CoEAs on combinatorial games, this result is a critical step towards a comprehensive theoretical framework for coevolution.

Introduction

Many of the most well-known games in the world are combinatorial games. Combinatorial games are typically perfect-information games played by two players without chance moves. The game has a finite number of possible positions, and players alternately take turns moving the game from one position to another, according to a set of rules describing which moves are legal. Combinatorial games are an exceptionally broad class of games, including famous games enjoyed the world over such as Chess or Go. Even those with simple rules can engender deep and complex strategic interactions between players. While this strategic depth is a key part of the appeal for human players, it can also render the task of computing a winning strategy to be extremely difficult. Indeed, games for which this task is known to be EXPTIME-complete (in terms of board size) include Chess [1], Go (with the ko rule) [2], and Checkers [3]. It is also known that determining an optimal strategy for a poset game (a class of combinatorial games which we will encounter in Section 6.4) is PSPACE-complete in terms of the size of the underlying poset [4]. (For further results, see [5].)

While classical methods are impractical for such cases, strong strategies can still be developed by using heuristic approaches, such as neural networks, Monte Carlo tree search, or genetic programming. Indeed, combinatorial games are a long-standing focus in the development of artificial intelligence, from Donald Michie’s seminal use of reinforcement learning on Tic-Tac-Toe [6], to Deep Blue’s famous matches against then-world Chess champion Garry Kasparov [7], to the recent groundbreaking results of DeepMind [8]. Many recent successes in this area train game playing agents using self-play, and among those self-play heuristics are coevolutionary algorithms (CoEAs) [9]. For a CoEA, self-play is realised through one or more evolving populations of individuals who compete against their contemporaries. In each iteration, the strongest individuals are selected based on their competitive interactions. Through genetic mutation and crossover, these strongest individuals are then used as parents for the individuals in the next iteration.

The successful application of CoEAs is deeply challenging, often due to the potential for games with intransitive payoff landscapes to induce cyclic behaviour [10]. For standard evolutionary algorithms, which apply similar methods to traditional optimisation problems, insight into how to avoid pathological behaviours can be provided by runtime analysis, which exists in great breadth and depth in literature and continues to be actively developed [11]. However, despite clear demand (see [9]), runtime analysis that addresses the challenges unique to CoEAs is far more limited. Indeed, while existing coevolutionary runtime analysis concerns a range of algorithms and design features, there are only three problem settings to which it so far applies: Bilinear, a game played on bitstrings whose outcome depends only on the number of 1-bits selected by each player [12–14]; Diagonal, a benchmark problem inspired by binary test-based optimisation [15]; and a class of symmetric zero-sum games with a payoff landscape that is globally very simple, but possibly locally intransitive [16]. Accordingly, our core research aim is to push the scope of runtime analysis for CoEAs towards games which feature more complex strategic interaction between players, and more closely reflect real-world games.

Motivated by the numerous empirical investigations into the topic (see Section 1.1), we focus our analysis on the use of CoEAs for combinatorial games, and in particular impartial combinatorial games. A combinatorial game is said to be impartial if both players share the same set of available moves at each game position [17]. It is common to also adopt the normal play convention, which assumes that a player loses if they have no legal moves available. For instance, consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SubtractionNim}_7^2$$\end{document}SUBTRACTIONNIM72 (formally introduced in Section 6.1), in which the game positions are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{0,1,2,3,4,5,6\}$$\end{document}{0,1,2,3,4,5,6} and a player must subtract either 1 or 2 from the position on their turn. A strategy may be encoded as a string of length 6, with entry i indicating whether a 1 or a 2 is to be subtracted when the game position is i. The main result of this paper (Theorem 5.2 and Corollary 5.5) is the first runtime analysis for a coevolutionary algorithm on impartial combinatorial games. In broad terms, it says the following.

Theorem 1.1

(Corollary 5.5, informal version) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}$$\end{document}A be the coevolutionary algorithm specified in Section 3, and let G be an impartial combinatorial game with n possible positions. Then, with high probability, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}$$\end{document}A discovers an optimal strategy for G within \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n^{O(\overline{s})}$$\end{document}nO(s¯) game evaluations, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}$$\end{document}s¯ is a precisely defined invariant of the corresponding game graph.

We note that the notion of a game graph is defined in Section 2 and the invariant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}$$\end{document}s¯ is defined in Section 4. For many games we find \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}=O(1)$$\end{document}s¯=O(1) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}=O(\log {n})$$\end{document}s¯=O(logn), and so this result implies a range of polynomial and quasipolynomial runtimes. While it appears likely that the upper bound provided is higher than the true runtime for specific games, a major strength is that it is immediately applicable to any impartial combinatorial game. As we also provide an easy method for bounding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}$$\end{document}s¯ above when its exact value is not obvious (see Proposition 4.4), deriving runtimes for well-known games is straightforward. Indeed, after distilling into a more concise form (Corollary 5.5), we will see applications to games including Nim, Silver Dollar, Turning Turtles, and Chomp.

To understand what is the significance of our result, it is helpful to first clarify what it is not. In no uncertain terms, this paper is not an account of a superior ready-to-use method for efficiently finding optimal strategies for combinatorial games. Strategies will here be encoded by exhaustively listing a preferred action for every possible game position, and thus the methods presented are necessarily at least linear in the number of game states, both in terms of memory and of time. With this naive representation, classical algorithms can already establish optimal strategies in time O(n) using Sprague-Grundy theory (see Section 2.1), which is best possible. However, many games are parameterised in such a way that the number of possible game positions grows exponentially (accordingly, we emphasise that Theorem 1.1 is not in contradiction with the aforementioned EXPTIME and PSPACE results). While the classical approach breaks down in such cases, a CoEA can still find success by replacing the exhaustive listing of actions with a model that maps features of the game position onto an action.

However, even when using the naive representation, our understanding of how to successfully apply CoEAs is very limited. If we wish to consistently apply CoEAs to advanced problems, whether they are rooted in game-playing or not, we must attain a comprehensive understanding of their behaviour in these simpler settings. Indeed, seemingly simple instances still produce payoff landscapes with features that make them difficult to optimise heuristically, such as intransitivity (as an example, in the already-introduced representation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SubtractionNim}_7^2$$\end{document}SUBTRACTIONNIM72, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\texttt {111121}$$\end{document}111121 defeats \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\texttt {122112}$$\end{document}122112, which in turn defeats \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\texttt {12122}$$\end{document}12122, which in turn defeats \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\texttt {111121}$$\end{document}111121, regardless of who plays first).

Thus, the main contribution of this paper is precisely this: a first step towards a theoretical understanding of CoEAs on combinatorial games. This greatly expands the scope of rigorous runtime analysis available for coevolution (which so far does not apply to any turn-based game, let alone combinatorial ones), and additionally complements the abundance of existing empirical analysis, which we review in Section 1.1. We emphasise that as this a first step, the analysis in this paper applies to a strategic representation using an exhaustive listing of actions, and this is not realistic for useful applications. Nonetheless, it remains a long term goal to push analysis towards more sophisticated representations, and insights into algorithm design and coevolutionary interactions gained here have utility towards that goal and for understanding coevolution in general. Furthermore, we believe our addition to the range of techniques available in this critical domain will in turn further the development of future runtime analysis of CoEAs.

We note here that the CoEA we analyse belongs to a class of evolutionary algorithms called estimation of distribution algorithms (EDAs) [18], which keep track of probability distributions over search spaces rather than explicit populations. A more comprehensive background on EDAs is given in Section 3. While there is a range of established analysis for non-coevolutionary EDAs (see [19]), the only prior analysis of a coevolutionary EDA is due to Benford and Lehre [16], who established an upper runtime bound on a simple class of symmetric zero-sum games. As the algorithm we analyse is a generalisation of the one appearing in [16], the proof of our main result adopts a similar high-level approach, which is to construct a function mapping the EDA’s probability distribution onto a single real number measuring progress towards optimality. This type of reduction is typical for analysis of EDAs in general (e.g., [20–24]), and is a feature of the broader family of techniques known as drift analysis [25]. However, for this approach to be successful, it is necessary to establish the constructed reduction does indeed have bias towards optimality, a fact that can only be derived by analysing how the game’s payoff function influences the progress of the algorithm. As this aspect is highly sensitive to the game being optimised, and this work presents the first analysis of CoEAs on combinatorial games, the proof features relevant to this aspect are novel technical advances (in particular, Lemma 3.2 characterising the distribution of parents selected using tournament selection on impartial combinatorial games, as well as the definition and analysis of the aforementioned parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}$$\end{document}s¯ in Section 4). We additionally note that the analysed EDA applies to multi-valued decision variables, rather than simply binary variables (this notion is also covered formally in Section 3). While not the main focus of this paper, there is only a small amount of preexisting analysis for EDAs operating over non-binary search domains, despite the clear utility of such algorithms. Our proof includes a detailed treatment of this setting, and may also provide methods useful in future analysis in this area.

The preliminary conference version [26] of this work presented a proof of the main result (Theorem 5.2) and applied it to a number of simple well-known combinatorial games (see Section 6). Here, we extend that version a number of ways. In Section 7 we consider in detail the strength of the main result, and identify key proof aspects and use cases for which closer analysis can lead to tighter runtime bounds. As part of this, we provide a new result (Lemma 7.1) demonstrating improvements of the main result are possible for games with simple optimal strategies. By applying the new result to the game Chomp (introduced in Section 6.4), we are able to improve the corresponding runtime bound from one that is quasipolynomial to one that is polynomial (and even to one that is polylogarithmic when choosing algorithmic parameters optimally). In addition, the implicit constant C in the main result (Theorem 5.2) is now given explicitly, and the corresponding proof is broken down using a new lemma in order to accommodate the new material.

In the remainder of this section, we review existing related work before stating notation. In Section 2 we give a more comprehensive discussion of impartial combinatorial games and review some Sprague-Grundy theory that will be relevant to our proof. In Section 3 we state the algorithm to which our result applies (UMDA), with an emphasis on its extension to multi-valued decision variables. In Section 4 we motivate and define the graph property \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}$$\end{document}s¯ appearing in Theorem 1.1, before then presenting the main result in Section 5. Following this, we apply the main result to a menagerie of selected impartial combinatorial games in Section 6. In Section 7 we identify how the main result can be improved using specialised analysis, and use such analysis to prove a stronger result for the game Chomp. Finally, concluding remarks and discussion of future directions is provided in Section 8.

Related work

Empirical analysis of coevolutionary algorithms for game playing. As game playing is a natural application for CoEAs, there have been a large number of empirical investigations into this topic, of which we can only list a fraction here. In one of the seminal and most well-known papers on competitive CoEAs (notable for introducing the ‘competitive fitness sharing’ evaluation method), Rosin and Belew [27] investigated design features for CoEAs optimising a 4-pile instance of Nim (an example of one of the intransitive combinatorial games analysed in this paper), noting that Nim was a difficult coevolutionary problem despite lending itself to simple crossover-friendly representations. Additionally, Jákowski, Krawiec, and Wieloch [28] observed in relation to experiments on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SubtractionNim}_{200}^3$$\end{document}SUBTRACTIONNIM2003 that intransitivity presents a strong challenge for CoEAs. CoEAs have also been studied in the context of non-impartial (yet still almost symmetric) combinatorial games. A pioneering work in this category is due to Pollack and Blair [29], who used a simple hillclimbing CoEA to evolve neural networks that evaluate board positions in Backgammon, a (non-deterministic) combinatorial game. The numerous subsequent studies that use CoEAs to evolve evaluators of board positions have considered combinatorial games including Othello [30–32], Senet [33], Checkers [34], Chess [35, 36], and Go [37]. CoEAs have also been used to approach games outside the scope of combinatorial games, including Pong [38], Bomberman [39], Poker [40], and Resistance [41]. Many competitive real-world interactions can be modelled as games, and CoEAs have been applied to such games arising from cybersecurity and defence [42, 43]. For a general survey of coevolution, see [44].

Runtime analysis of coevolutionary algorithms. Until recently, the only existing coevolutionary runtime analysis result, due to Jansen and Wiegand [45], applied to a cooperative coevolutionary algorithm, which uses multiple populations to collectively solve traditional optimisation problems. The present work considers competitive coevolutionary algorithms, which apply to game-theoretic scenarios by evolving populations of individuals which are evaluated against contemporary adversaries. The first runtime analysis applicable to competitive coevolution was established by Lehre [12], who showed that a population-based CoEA which selects using a pairwise dominance relation is able to approximate the Nash equilibrium of instances of a game called \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Bilinear}$$\end{document}BILINEAR in expected polynomial time. A key theoretical insight into algorithm design from the same paper was the identification of an error threshold for mutation rate, above which no CoEA can efficiently optimise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Bilinear}$$\end{document}BILINEAR. Further runtime analysis for CoEAs on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Bilinear}$$\end{document}BILINEAR has concerned the roles played by fitness aggregation methods [13] and archives [14] in algorithm behaviour. Inspired by promising applications of CoEAs for optimising binary test-based problems, Lin and Lehre [15] provided runtime analysis establishing the benefit of using a CoEA over a traditional EA for optimising a benchmark problem called \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Diagonal}$$\end{document}DIAGONAL. In [16], Benford and Lehre considered the importance of maintaining a diverse set of opponents when coevolving game strategies, showing that any CoEA able to retain only one individual between generations cannot efficiently find optimal strategies on a certain class of symmetric zero-sum games. In the positive direction, it is shown in [16] that the coevolutionary estimation of distribution algorithm also studied in this paper (see Section 3) discovers the optimal strategy of games belonging to the same class in polynomial time.

We use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Int}(G)=\{v\in V:F(v)\ne \emptyset \}$$\end{document}Int(G)={v∈V:F(v)≠∅} to denote the set of non-sink vertices of G (the interior vertices). A reverse topological ordering for G is an ordering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1,\ldots ,u_n$$\end{document}u1,…,un of V such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(u_i)\subseteq \{u_1,\ldots ,u_{i-1}\}$$\end{document}F(ui)⊆{u1,…,ui-1} holds for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in [n]$$\end{document}i∈[n] (note that such an ordering always exists as G is assumed to be acyclic).

All logarithms are the natural logarithm unless stated otherwise, and given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\log ^k{n}=(\log {n})^k$$\end{document}logkn=(logn)k.

Impartial Combinatorial Games

Let us briefly review the representation of impartial games via directed graphs and some Sprague-Grundy theory (see, for example, [46, 47]). An impartial combinatorial game is a finite acyclic rooted directed graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,F,v_0)$$\end{document}G=(V,F,v0) (see Section 1.2), where V is a vertex set of size n, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_0\in V$$\end{document}v0∈V is the initial game position. Players take it in turns to move the current position to one of its out-neighbours. We adopt the convention that if a player is unable to make a move because the current position has no out-neighbours (i.e., it is a sink), then that player loses. This is usually referred to as the normal play convention. We will also always assume that for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V, there is a directed path from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_0$$\end{document}v0 to v, so that every game position is reachable.

We will encode strategies for impartial combinatorial games as an assignment of each non-sink game position v to an element of F(v) (that is, an out-neighbour of v), with this assignment indicating the preferred move at each game position. Formally, recalling that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Int}(G)$$\end{document}Int(G) denotes the set of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(v)\ne \emptyset $$\end{document}F(v)≠∅, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {X}_G=\prod _{v\in \text {Int}(G)}F(v) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}XG=∏v∈Int(G)F(v)will be the set of strategies for G. Note that an element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x∈XG may be regarded as a mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {{Int}}(G)\rightarrow V$$\end{document}Int(G)→V, and so we will write x(v) for the image of a position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V under this mapping. This formulation coincides closely with that featured in the aforementioned work of Richie on reinforcement learning for optimal Tic-Tac-Toe play [6], and has similarities to subsequent ‘move selector’ representations which identify a preferred action based on the current game position using, for example, genetic programming [39], neural networks [37, 38], or a game-specific mapping [40, 41]. However, it stands distinct from ‘state evaluator’ representations which play by evaluating board positions, whether by recording evaluations for all possible positions [27, 28], genetic programming [33, 36, 48], neural networks [29, 32, 34, 35], or otherwise.

As is typical for the uses of coevolution for gameplaying discussed in Section 1.1, players receive a payoff depending only on whether the final outcome of the game was win or lose. Accordingly, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G:\mathcal {X}_G\times \mathcal {X}_G\rightarrow \{-1,1\}$$\end{document}fG:XG×XG→{-1,1} be the payoff function for G, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G(x,y)=1$$\end{document}fG(x,y)=1 indicates that x wins against y and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G(x,y)=-1$$\end{document}fG(x,y)=-1 indicates that x loses against y (where x makes the first move). Precisely, if we recursively define for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_G^v(x,y)= {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} -f_G^{x(v)}(y,x) & \qquad \text { if } v\in \text {Int}(G),\\ -1 & \qquad \text {otherwise,} \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fGv(x,y)=-fGx(v)(y,x)ifv∈Int(G),-1otherwise,then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G(x,y)=f_G^{v_0}(x,y)$$\end{document}fG(x,y)=fG0(x,y). It will also be convenient to define for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x,y∈XG,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {{Path}}_{G}(x,y)=\{v_0,x(v_0),y(x(v_0)),x(y(x(v_0))),\ldots \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PathG(x,y)={v0,x(v0),y(x(v0)),x(y(x(v0))),…}.We will always assume that there is some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x∈XG such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G(x,y)=1$$\end{document}fG(x,y)=1 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}y∈XG (i.e., that the first player has a winning strategy for G). Indeed, if this is not the case, then the second player has a winning strategy, and so we can add a fictitious initial position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^*$$\end{document}v∗ to G with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(v^*)=\{v_0\}$$\end{document}F(v∗)={v0} to obtain a game equally challenging as G but with a winning strategy for the first player. We thus define the set of optimal strategies for G to be\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Opt}(G)=\{x\in \mathcal {X}_G:f_G(x,y)=1 \text { for every } y\in \mathcal {X}\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Opt(G)={x∈XG:fG(x,y)=1for everyy∈X},and remark that the above assumption implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Opt}(G)$$\end{document}Opt(G) will always be non-empty.

The Sprague-Grundy Function

First introduced independently by Sprague [49, 50] and Grundy [51], the Sprague-Grundy function of an impartial combinatorial game is a function mapping game positions onto non-negative integers, which contains information about the game’s strategic landscape and how optimal play is affected when building new games out of smaller ones [52]. Formally, given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,F,v_0)$$\end{document}G=(V,F,v0), the Sprague-Grundy function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:V\rightarrow \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}h:V→N0 is defined recursively. First, all sink vertices are given the value 0. Then, once all out-neighbours of v have a value assigned, we define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} h(v)=\text {{mex}}\,{\{h(w):w\in F(v)\}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}h(v)=mex{h(w):w∈F(v)},where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {{mex}}\,{S}=\min {(\mathbb {N}_0\setminus S)}$$\end{document}mexS=min(N0\S) denotes the smallest non-negative number not in a finite set S (the ‘minimum excluded integer’). We call h(v) the Grundy value of the position v.

Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V, if the current position is v then the player making the next move has a winning strategy if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(v)\ne 0$$\end{document}h(v)≠0. Accordingly, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(v)=0$$\end{document}h(v)=0 then the player making the next move will always lose against an opponent who plays optimally. Thus, victory can be assured for the player making the first move by always choosing to move to vertices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h^{-1}(\{0\})$$\end{document}h-1({0}). Because this happens automatically whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(v)\setminus h^{-1}(\{0\})=\emptyset $$\end{document}F(v)\h-1({0})=∅, an optimal strategy can be guaranteed by learning optimal moves at a set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_G$$\end{document}WG (which we refer to as critical positions) defined in the following way.

Definition 2.1

Given an impartial combinatorial game \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,F,v_0)$$\end{document}G=(V,F,v0), let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W_G=\{v\in \text {{Int}}(G):h(v)\ne 0 \text { and } F(v)\setminus h^{-1}(\{0\})\ne \emptyset \}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}WG={v∈Int(G):h(v)≠0andF(v)\h-1({0})≠∅},where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:V\rightarrow \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}h:V→N0 is the Sprague-Grundy function for G.

Because any strategy that makes a correct move at each critical position is automatically an optimal strategy, our proofs will essentially analyse how long it takes for a CoEA to learn the correct move at each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in W_G$$\end{document}v∈WG. The following lemma formalises the fact that optimal play is assured by learning correct moves at the critical positions in a more general form that will be useful to quote later.

Lemma 2.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:V\rightarrow \mathbb {N}_0$$\end{document}h:V→N0 denote the Sprague-Grundy function of a combinatorial game G. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1,\ldots ,u_n$$\end{document}u1,…,un be a reverse topological ordering for G. Then the following properties hold for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in [n]$$\end{document}i∈[n]. A1If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(u_i)\ne 0$$\end{document}h(ui)≠0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x∈XG satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(x(v))=0$$\end{document}h(x(v))=0 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in W_G\cap \{u_1,\ldots ,u_i\}$$\end{document}v∈WG∩{u1,…,ui}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G^{u_i}(x,y)=1$$\end{document}fGi(x,y)=1 holds for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}y∈XG.A2If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(u_i)=0$$\end{document}h(ui)=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}y∈XG satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(y(v))=0$$\end{document}h(y(v))=0 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in W_G\cap \{u_1,\ldots ,u_i\}$$\end{document}v∈WG∩{u1,…,ui}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G^{u_i}(x,y)=-1$$\end{document}fGi(x,y)=-1 holds for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x∈XG. In particular, with our assumption that the first player always has a winning strategy for G, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x∈XG satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(x(v))=0$$\end{document}h(x(v))=0 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in W_G$$\end{document}v∈WG, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \text {Opt}(G)$$\end{document}x∈Opt(G).

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(u_i)\ne 0$$\end{document}h(ui)≠0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x∈XG satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(x(v))=0$$\end{document}h(x(v))=0 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in W_G\cap \{u_1,\ldots ,u_i\}$$\end{document}v∈WG∩{u1,…,ui}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G^{u_i}(x,y)=1$$\end{document}fGi(x,y)=1 holds for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}y∈XG.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(u_i)=0$$\end{document}h(ui)=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}y∈XG satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(y(v))=0$$\end{document}h(y(v))=0 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in W_G\cap \{u_1,\ldots ,u_i\}$$\end{document}v∈WG∩{u1,…,ui}, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G^{u_i}(x,y)=-1$$\end{document}fGi(x,y)=-1 holds for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x∈XG.

Proof

We prove that the conditions A1 and A2 always hold by induction on i. For the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1$$\end{document}i=1, note that we must have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(u_1)=0$$\end{document}h(u1)=0 (as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_i\notin \text {Int}(G)$$\end{document}ui∉Int(G)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G^{u_1}(x,y)=-1$$\end{document}fG1(x,y)=-1 for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x,y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x,y∈XG. For the inductive stage, there are two cases to consider. First, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(u_i)=0$$\end{document}h(ui)=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}y∈XG satisfies Note that the final conclusion of Lemma 2.2 is a sufficient condition, but not a necessary condition, as demonstrated by Figure 1.Fig. 1In the combinatorial game illustrated here, Sprague-Grundy values at each game position are shown in red. In this game, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_G=\{v_0,b\}$$\end{document}WG={v0,b}. However, any strategy x with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x(v_0)=d$$\end{document}x(v0)=d is automatically optimal (the first player wins on their first turn), and so the condition of Lemma 2.2 is not a necessary one

In the combinatorial game illustrated here, Sprague-Grundy values at each game position are shown in red. In this game, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_G=\{v_0,b\}$$\end{document}WG={v0,b}. However, any strategy x with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x(v_0)=d$$\end{document}x(v0)=d is automatically optimal (the first player wins on their first turn), and so the condition of Lemma 2.2 is not a necessary one

Algorithm

Rather than storing a population as a set of points in the search space, as is the case for most EAs, an estimation of distribution algorithm (EDA) represents its population as a probability distribution over the search space [18]. Whereas most algorithms sample candidates for selection from their current population uniformly at random, an EDA instead samples from its probability distribution. After selection has been completed, the selected individuals are then used to update the probability distribution for the next generation. Much of the existing runtime analysis for EDAs (see [22–24, 53, 54]) has emphasised the benefit provided by a high level of diversity among generated search points. This is also the case in the recent first runtime analysis of a coevolutionary EDA [16], wherein the difficulty presented by locally intransitive payoff landscapes could be provably averted by evaluating strategies against a diverse set of opponents. As intransitivity is also apparent in impartial combinatorial games, a coevolutionary EDA is a good candidate for a first runtime analysis on this topic too.

While highly sophisticated EDAs may account for correlations between sampled variables, the simplest EDAs operating over bitstrings are univariate, in the sense that they represent a distribution as a simple vector of uncorrelated ‘bit frequencies’ \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(p_1,\ldots ,p_n)\in [0,1]^n$$\end{document}(p1,…,pn)∈[0,1]n. To generate a candidate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \{0,1\}^n$$\end{document}x∈{0,1}n for selection, each bit x(i) is sampled independently according to a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Bernoulli}(p_i)$$\end{document}Bernoulli(pi) random variable. EDAs adopting this regime include cGA [55] and PBIL [56]. However, probably the most extensively studied univariate EDA is the Univariate Marginal Distribution Algorithm (UMDA) [57, 58]. Given algorithmic parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \leqslant \lambda $$\end{document}μ⩽λ, UMDA typically generates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ individuals in this way, retains the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ candidates with highest fitness, and for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in [n]$$\end{document}i∈[n] defines the next bit frequency \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_i$$\end{document}pi to be equal to relative frequency of 1-bits among those fittest \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ individuals. UMDA captures the defining mechanism of EDAs, yet its simplicity has enabled rigorous proofs of efficient runtime on classical benchmarks including LeadingOnes and OneMax (see, for example, [53]), making it an important algorithm for foundational analysis. See [19] for a general survey of theoretical analysis of UMDA and other EDAs.

The algorithm we analyse is a coevolutionary variant of UMDA, in which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\mu $$\end{document}2μ individuals are generated and paired off to play against each other, with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ winners of those games being used to determine the frequencies for the next generation. While UMDA is commonly formulated with respect to bitstrings, our setting relies on a more general search domain. As outlined in Section 2, for a parent set S, we are considering search domains of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}=\prod _{i\in I}S_i$$\end{document}X=∏i∈ISi, where I is an indexing set and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_i\subseteq S$$\end{document}Si⊆S for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in I$$\end{document}i∈I. Given a tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \prod _{i\in I}\mathcal {P}(S_i)$$\end{document}p∈∏i∈IP(Si), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {{Univ}}(\mathcal {X},p)$$\end{document}Univ(X,p) denote the probability distribution over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X such that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\sim \text {{Univ}}(\mathcal {X},p)$$\end{document}x∼Univ(X,p) then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathcal \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}(x=y)=\prod _{i\in I}p(i)(y_i), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(x=y)=∏i∈Ip(i)(yi),so that the distribution of x is that of an independent univariate sampling for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in I$$\end{document}i∈I. For notational convenience, given a tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \prod _{i\in I}\mathcal {P}(S_i)$$\end{document}p∈∏i∈IP(Si) we will often write for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in I$$\end{document}i∈I and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in S$$\end{document}s∈S,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} p(i,s)= {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} p(i)(s)& \qquad \text { if } s\in S_i,\\ 0& \qquad \text {otherwise.} \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}p(i,s)=p(i)(s)ifs∈Si,0otherwise.Our algorithm will represent its current population as an element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \prod _{i\in I}\mathcal {P}(S_i)$$\end{document}p∈∏i∈IP(Si), with individuals being generated according to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {{Univ}}(\mathcal {X},p)$$\end{document}Univ(X,p). In the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I=[n]$$\end{document}I=[n] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_i=\{0,1\}$$\end{document}Si={0,1} for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in [n]$$\end{document}i∈[n], we recover the standard framework for univariate EDAs operating over bitstrings. For these EDAs, the tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \prod _{i\in [n]}\mathcal {P}(\{0,1\})$$\end{document}p∈∏i∈[n]P({0,1}) is often represented as a frequency vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(p(1),\ldots ,p(n))\in [0,1]^n$$\end{document}(p(1),…,p(n))∈[0,1]n, where p(i) is the probability that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\sim \text {{Univ}}(\{0,1\}^n,p)$$\end{document}x∼Univ({0,1}n,p) has a 1-bit in position i. A common feature for EDAs operating over bitstrings is to constrain these frequencies to the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\gamma ,1-\gamma ]$$\end{document}[γ,1-γ] for some small \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ at the end of each generation. For the general case, where we track a tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \prod _{i\in I}\mathcal {P}(S_i)$$\end{document}p∈∏i∈IP(Si), we need to constrain each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(i)\in \mathcal {P}(S_i)$$\end{document}p(i)∈P(Si) to the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_\gamma (S_i)$$\end{document}Pγ(Si). To achieve this, we adopt the following minor variation of the multi-valued EDA framework proposed by Ben Jedidia, Doerr, and Krejca [59]. Given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in [0,\textstyle {\frac{1}{|S|}})$$\end{document}γ∈[0,1|S|) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \mathcal {P}(S)$$\end{document}p∈P(S), let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta ^+_\gamma (p)=\sum _{s\in S}\max {\{p(s)-\gamma ,0\}},\qquad \beta ^-_\gamma (p)=\sum _{s\in S}\max {\{\gamma -p(s),0\}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}βγ+(p)=∑s∈Smax{p(s)-γ,0},βγ-(p)=∑s∈Smax{γ-p(s),0}.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _\gamma ^S:\mathcal {P}(S)\rightarrow \mathcal {P}_\gamma (S)$$\end{document}πγS:P(S)→Pγ(S) then be the function given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}πγS(p)(s)=γifp(s)⩽γ,γ+1-βγ-(p)βγ+(p)(p(s)-γ)ifp(s)⩾γ.For the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|S|=2$$\end{document}|S|=2 the definition reduces to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _\gamma ^S(p)(s)=\min {\{\max {\{p(s),\gamma \}},1-\gamma \}}$$\end{document}πγS(p)(s)=min{max{p(s),γ},1-γ}, and so this model fits the usual method for constraining univariate EDAs over bitstrings.

Despite some differences in notation, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _\gamma ^S$$\end{document}πγS is nearly identical the restriction described in [59]. In the context of [59, Section 4.2], our only modification is to forego an initial clamping of probabilities to the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\gamma ,1-(|S|-1)\gamma ]$$\end{document}[γ,1-(|S|-1)γ], as the upper border of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-(|S|-1)\gamma $$\end{document}1-(|S|-1)γ is already implied by the fact that the remaining steps produce an element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_\gamma (S)$$\end{document}Pγ(S). Indeed, an actual difference between the two methods only arises for inputs p satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\max _{s\in S}p(s)>1-(|S|-1)\gamma $$\end{document}maxs∈Sp(s)>1-(|S|-1)γ, and even in such cases the difference is not significant.

The fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _\gamma ^S$$\end{document}πγS always outputs an element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {P}_\gamma (S)$$\end{document}Pγ(S) is verified by B1 in the following lemma, which also establishes several further properties of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _\gamma $$\end{document}πγ which will be useful for our later proofs.

Lemma 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _\gamma ^+$$\end{document}βγ+, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _\gamma ^-$$\end{document}βγ-, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _\gamma ^S$$\end{document}πγS be as defined in Section 3. Then, the following properties hold. B1For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \mathcal {P}(S)$$\end{document}p∈P(S), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sum _{s\in S}\pi _\gamma ^S(p)(s)=1$$\end{document}∑s∈SπγS(p)(s)=1.B2If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(s)\geqslant \gamma $$\end{document}p(s)⩾γ, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( 1-\textstyle {\frac{\beta _\gamma ^-(p)}{1-\gamma |S|}}\right) p(s)\leqslant \pi _\gamma ^S(p)(s)\leqslant p(s)$$\end{document}1-βγ-(p)1-γ|S|p(s)⩽πγS(p)(s)⩽p(s).B3For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} A description of the version of UMDA we analyse is now provided by Algorithm 1, which effectively generalises the version appearing in [16] (which applied only to bitstrings and omitted the step involving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _\gamma ^{S_i}$$\end{document}πγi).
