In this section we will apply Theorem 5.2 to obtain several runtimes for Algorithm 1 on a number of well-established combinatorial games. Throughout, we state runtimes in terms of n, the number of possible game positions, and always assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}$$\end{document}A is described by Algorithm 1. All described games are played under the normal play convention (that a player unable to move loses), as established in Section 2.

Subtraction Nim

Nim is a strategic game in which players take turns removing items from distinct heaps. Variants have been played across cultures since ancient history [63, 64], and it was also the game of choice for some of the earliest machines and computers dedicated to game playing [65–67]. Nim is also perhaps the most important impartial combinatorial game from a mathematical perspective, with the Sprague-Grundy theorem establishing that, for a particular formulation of equivalence which characterises strategic continuation, every position in any impartial combinatorial game is equivalent to some position of a one-heap game of Nim [68].

While the version central to combinatorial game theory typically allows players to remove any positive number of items on their turn, here we consider the well-known one-heap variant in which there is an upper limit on the number of items that can be taken at once (see, for example, [52]). Given parameters n and k, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SubtractionNim}_n^k$$\end{document}SUBTRACTIONNIMnk begins with an initial heap of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n-1)$$\end{document}(n-1) items, and on each turn a player may remove between 1 and k items from the heap. The game graph for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SubtractionNim}_n^2$$\end{document}SUBTRACTIONNIMn2 is shown in Figure 3, where we identify the size of the heap to be decreasing as we move from left to right. This game constitutes the simplest example of a subtraction game [17] of also a take-away game [69], both of which are expansive and well-studied classes of impartial combinatorial games. We have the following polynomial runtime for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SubtractionNim}_n^k$$\end{document}SUBTRACTIONNIMnk.

Proposition 6.1

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SubtractionNim} _n^k$$\end{document}SUBTRACTIONNIMnk satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}\leqslant 1$$\end{document}s¯⩽1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \leqslant k$$\end{document}Δ⩽k. Thus, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K>0$$\end{document}K>0 there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C'>0$$\end{document}C′>0 such that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ are chosen as in Corollary 5.5,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}[T_\mathcal {A}(\text {{Opt}}(\textsc {SubtractionNim} _n^k))\geqslant C'(kn)^5\log ^2{n}]\leqslant n^{-K}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TA(Opt(SUBTRACTIONNIMnk))⩾C′(kn)5log2n]⩽n-K.

Proof

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SubtractionNim}_n^k$$\end{document}SUBTRACTIONNIMnk we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V=\{0,1,\ldots ,n-1\}$$\end{document}V={0,1,…,n-1}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_0=n-1$$\end{document}v0=n-1, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F(v)=\{v-1,\ldots ,v-k\}\cap V$$\end{document}F(v)={v-1,…,v-k}∩V. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\setminus \text {Int}(G)=\{0\}$$\end{document}V\Int(G)={0}.

We need to verify that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s(v)\leqslant 1$$\end{document}s(v)⩽1 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V. Given v, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_v=\{(v+i,v):i\in [k]\}\cap E(G)$$\end{document}Av={(v+i,v):i∈[k]}∩E(G), so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_v$$\end{document}Av consists of all edges directed towards v (as shown in Figure 3). We have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Depth}(A_v)=1$$\end{document}Depth(Av)=1, as any directed path in G can visit v at most once. To see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_v$$\end{document}Av is a v-switcher, suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0\ldots z_\ell $$\end{document}z0…zℓ is an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_v$$\end{document}Av-compatible directed path from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0=n-1$$\end{document}z0=n-1 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\setminus \text {Int}(G)=\{0\}$$\end{document}V\Int(G)={0}. Because at most k items are removed on each turn, there is some i such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i\in \{v,v+1,\ldots ,v+(k-1)\}$$\end{document}zi∈{v,v+1,…,v+(k-1)} (that is, the game must reach a position where it is possible to move to v within one step using an edge in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_v$$\end{document}Av). But then we either have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_i=v$$\end{document}zi=v or, in order for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_0\ldots z_{i+1}$$\end{document}z0…zi+1 to remain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_v$$\end{document}Av-compatible, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_iz_{i+1}\in A_v$$\end{document}zizi+1∈Av and hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_{i+1}=v$$\end{document}zi+1=v. In either case, we deduce that v lies on every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_v$$\end{document}Av-compatible directed path from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n-1$$\end{document}n-1 to 0. Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_v$$\end{document}Av is a v-switcher of depth 1, and hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s(v)\leqslant 1$$\end{document}s(v)⩽1.

From this, we have that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}\leqslant 1$$\end{document}s¯⩽1. Combined with the observation that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \leqslant k$$\end{document}Δ⩽k, the result then follows from Corollary 5.5. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Silver Dollar

We consider the variant of Silver Dollar played without the eponymous silver dollar [17, 68, 70]; however, it should be noted that Theorem 5.2 also implies a similar polynomial runtime for the original version of Silver Dollar attributed to de Bruijn (see also [68]).

Given parameters m and k, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SilverDollar}_m^k$$\end{document}SILVERDOLLARmk is played using k coins on a horizontal strip of m squares, with the coins initially placed on the rightmost k squares (most descriptions actually have the coins placed on arbitrary starting squares, however this does not significantly affect our analysis). A turn consists of moving one coin leftwards any number of spaces, provided the coin does not go past any other coins. In addition, coins may never occupy the same square. Assuming k is a fixed constant, the number of game positions is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=\left( {\begin{array}{c}m\\ k\end{array}}\right) $$\end{document}n=mk. We have the following polynomial runtime for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SilverDollar}_m^k$$\end{document}SILVERDOLLARmk.

Proposition 6.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}k∈N be fixed. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {SilverDollar} _m^k$$\end{document}SILVERDOLLARmk satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}\leqslant k$$\end{document}s¯⩽k and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \leqslant m=O(n^{1/k})$$\end{document}Δ⩽m=O(n1/k). Thus, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K>0$$\end{document}K>0 there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C'>0$$\end{document}C′>0 such that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ are chosen as in Corollary 5.5,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}[T_\mathcal {A}(\text {{Opt}}(\textsc {SilverDollar} _m^k))\geqslant C'n^{5+3k+(2/k)}\log ^2{n}]\leqslant n^{-K}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TA(Opt(SILVERDOLLARmk))⩾C′n5+3k+(2/k)log2n]⩽n-K.

Proof

On each turn, for each empty square there is at most one possible move that places a coin onto that square. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \leqslant m-k\leqslant m=O(n^{1/k})$$\end{document}Δ⩽m-k⩽m=O(n1/k). Next, any possible game position can be reached from the starting position in at most k moves (simply move each coin in order from left to right onto the required square). Therefore, using Proposition 4.4, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}\leqslant k$$\end{document}s¯⩽k. The required result then follows from Corollary 5.5 using these bounds on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta $$\end{document}Δ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}$$\end{document}s¯. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Turning Turtles

Here we consider one instance of a large class of coin turning games [17, 71]. Given a parameter m, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {TurningTurtles}_m$$\end{document}TURNINGTURTLESm is played using a row of m coins, initially all showing heads. A turn consists of turning over one coin from heads to tails, and then optionally turning over one more coin anywhere to the left of that one (regardless of whether it is showing heads or tails). Play continues until all coins show tails. Noting that the total number of game positions is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2^m$$\end{document}n=2m, we have the following quasipolynomial runtime for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {TurningTurtles}_m$$\end{document}TURNINGTURTLESm.

Proposition 6.3

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {TurningTurtles} _m$$\end{document}TURNINGTURTLESm satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}\leqslant \log _2{n}$$\end{document}s¯⩽log2n and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \leqslant (\log _2{n})^2$$\end{document}Δ⩽(log2n)2. Thus, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K>0$$\end{document}K>0 there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0$$\end{document}c>0 such that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ are chosen as in Corollary 5.5,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}[T_\mathcal {A}(\text {{Opt}}(\textsc {TurningTurtles} _m))\geqslant n^{c\log {n}}]\leqslant n^{-K}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TA(Opt(TURNINGTURTLESm))⩾nclogn]⩽n-K.

Proof

On each turn, there are at most m possible moves that turn over only one coin and at most \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( {\begin{array}{c}m\\ 2\end{array}}\right) $$\end{document}m2 possible moves that turn over two coins. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \leqslant m+\left( {\begin{array}{c}m\\ 2\end{array}}\right) \leqslant m^2$$\end{document}Δ⩽m+m2⩽m2. Next, any possible game position can be reached from the starting position in at most m moves (simply turn over the required coins from heads to tails one by one). Therefore, using Proposition 4.4 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}\leqslant m$$\end{document}s¯⩽m. Noting that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=\log _2{n}$$\end{document}m=log2n, and hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&C^2(K+\overline{s}+1)(20\Delta n)^{2+3\overline{s}}\log ^2{n}\leqslant C^2(K+\log _2{n}+1)(20(\log _2{n})^2n)^{2+3\log _2{n}}\log ^2{n}\\&\quad \leqslant n^{c\log {n}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C2(K+s¯+1)(20Δn)2+3s¯log2n⩽C2(K+log2n+1)(20(log2n)2n)2+3log2nlog2n⩽nclogn,the required result then follows from Corollary 5.5. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Chomp

Since its introduction by Schuh [72] and later by Gale [73], Chomp has inspired a great deal of theoretical and empirical analysis, as well as numerous variants incorporating, for example, graphs and simplicial complexes [74]. While the optimal strategy is not known in general, a strategy-stealing argument shows that the first player always has a winning strategy for Chomp [73].

Given parameters k and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell $$\end{document}ℓ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Chomp}_{k,\ell }$$\end{document}CHOMPk,ℓ is played on an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\times \ell $$\end{document}k×ℓ board. A turn consists of removing one square, as well as all squares to the right and above. However, if a player removes the square in the lower-left corner (the ‘poison’ square) they immediately lose. Note that to instantiate this game under our normal play convention, we can make removing the lower-left corner fatal by simply removing the position that has no remaining squares. We can establish the following quasipolynomial runtime for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Chomp}_{k,\ell }$$\end{document}CHOMPk,ℓ.

Proposition 6.4

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Chomp} _{k,\ell }$$\end{document}CHOMPk,ℓ satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}\leqslant O(\log _2{n})$$\end{document}s¯⩽O(log2n) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \leqslant n$$\end{document}Δ⩽n. Thus, for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K>0$$\end{document}K>0 there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0$$\end{document}c>0 such that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ are chosen as in Corollary 5.5,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}[T_\mathcal {A}(\text {{Opt}}(\textsc {Chomp} _{k,\ell }))\geqslant n^{c\log {n}}]\leqslant n^{-K}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TA(Opt(CHOMPk,ℓ))⩾nclogn]⩽n-K.

Proof

Without loss of generality assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\leqslant \ell $$\end{document}k⩽ℓ, so that the initial board has at least as many columns as rows. In each possible game position, every row must be at least as long as the row above it. In particular, there is a correspondence between game positions and lattice paths (i.e., paths that only move right and down along the squares’ edges) from the top-left corner to bottom-right corner, with the path marking out the boundary of the remaining squares. Using stars and bars counting (see [75, Theorem 8.5.1] for a full treatment) and removing the position that has no remaining squares, the total number of game positions is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=\left( {\begin{array}{c}k+\ell \\ k\end{array}}\right) -1\geqslant \left( {\begin{array}{c}2k\\ k\end{array}}\right) -1=\Theta (4^k/\sqrt{k})$$\end{document}n=k+ℓk-1⩾2kk-1=Θ(4k/k). Trivially, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \leqslant n$$\end{document}Δ⩽n. Next, any possible game position can be reached from the starting position in at most k moves (simply make the appropriate chomp row by row working from top to bottom). Therefore, using Proposition 4.4, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{s}\leqslant k$$\end{document}s¯⩽k. Noting that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\leqslant \alpha \log {n}$$\end{document}k⩽αlogn for some constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α, and hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C^2(K+\overline{s}+1)(20\Delta n)^{2+3\overline{s}}\log ^2{n}\leqslant C^2(K+\alpha \log {n}+1)(20n^2)^{2+3\alpha \log {n}}\log ^2{n}\leqslant n^{c\log {n}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C2(K+s¯+1)(20Δn)2+3s¯log2n⩽C2(K+αlogn+1)(20n2)2+3αlognlog2n⩽nclogn,the required result then follows from Corollary 5.5. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Improvements to The General Runtime Bound

Theorem 5.2 and Corollary 5.5 provide highly general runtime bounds that are applicable to any combinatorial game. However, in order to accommodate this level of generality, a number of broad assumptions are necessary in the corresponding proofs, resulting in a potential lack of tightness for specific games. In this section we highlight some of the more pessimistic proof steps and consider how each could be addressed, including a demonstration of how a specialised analysis can prove a stronger runtime for Chomp when played on a square board.

At a high level, the proof of Theorem 5.2 shows that it is possible to discover optimal play at each critical position of a game in a sequence that is faithful to an arbitrary topological ordering \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1,\ldots ,u_n$$\end{document}u1,…,un for the game. One critical assumption is that if v is the next critical position to be optimised, or one that has already been learned, then the probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}(v\in \text {{Path}}_{G}(x,y))$$\end{document}P(v∈PathG(x,y)) that v is encountered in a game played out by sampled individuals x, y is bound below by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{s(v)}$$\end{document}γs(v) (this bound is established by Lemma 4.5 and subsequently used to deduce (21)). Lemma 3.2 demonstrates that analysis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}(v\in \text {{Path}}_{G}(x,y))$$\end{document}P(v∈PathG(x,y)) is a major contribution to the eventual runtime, serving a role akin to a dynamic learning rate for the algorithm at position v. A key insight is that encountering a large range of game positions by evaluating diverse sets of opponents is essential to an algorithm’s success. However, with further specification of the game graph, the general bound \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}(v\in \text {{Path}}_{G}(x,y))\geqslant \gamma ^{s(v)}$$\end{document}P(v∈PathG(x,y))⩾γs(v) can be greatly improved through closer analysis of coevolutionary dynamics. For example, if individuals often misplay at a winning position v, opponents should begin to exploit this by steering the game towards v; the resulting feedback mechanism between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}(v\in \text {{Path}}_{G}(x,y))$$\end{document}P(v∈PathG(x,y)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_t(v,\,\cdot \,)$$\end{document}pt(v,·) can assist more efficient learning. This type of analysis is used in [76] to establish a runtime of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(n^2\log ^3{n})$$\end{document}O(n2log3n) on a Nim-like impartial combinatorial game called Reciprocal LeadingOnes, improving the more naive upper bound of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O(n^5\log ^2{n})$$\end{document}O(n5log2n) implied by Theorem 5.2 (in fact, [76] also provides experimental results for Reciprocal LeadingOnes and other combinatorial games suggesting the true upper bound may be asymptotically even smaller).Fig. 4A game that should be easy to optimise, but contains vertices with switchability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta (n)$$\end{document}Θ(n)

A game that should be easy to optimise, but contains vertices with switchability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta (n)$$\end{document}Θ(n)

A potential lack of tightness can also be implied by the assumption in the proof that the order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1,\ldots ,u_n$$\end{document}u1,…,un in which game positions are optimised is a reverse topological ordering. While this shows that for any game there always exists at least one ordering of critical positions conducive to sequential learning of optimal play, there is no guarantee that a reverse topological ordering will be the most efficient ordering or the one that arises in practice. While a reverse topological ordering is probably most efficient for certain games (such as Subtraction Nim), in other cases proving stronger runtime bounds should be possible by considering an alternative ordering of vertices. As an example, consider Figure 4. Any player who choses to move to u from the top row is guaranteed to lose, and so in light of Lemma 3.2 there is always selective pressure towards optimal play at each position v, with the strength of that pressure scaling with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}(v\in \text {{Path}}_{G}(x,y))$$\end{document}P(v∈PathG(x,y)). However, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}(v\in \text {{Path}}_{G}(x,y))$$\end{document}P(v∈PathG(x,y)) is necessarily greater for vertices towards the left of the top row, and so these positions will be optimised by UMDA first, contrary to the reverse topological ordering which goes from right to left. Even though this game should be straightforward for UMDA to optimise (that is, polynomial in n runtime), the bound arising from Theorem 5.2 is particularly pessimistic due to the existence of vertices (towards the right of the top row) with switchability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta (n)$$\end{document}Θ(n).

Stronger Runtime Bounds for Games with Simple Optimal Strategies

The proof of Theorem 5.2 considers the time until UMDA generates an individual who plays optimally at every critical position of the game, as Lemma 2.2 proves that this is a sufficient condition for membership of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {{Opt}}(G)$$\end{document}Opt(G). However, because Lemma 2.2 is not a necessary condition, there is potential for CoEAs to demonstrate bias towards learning simpler elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Opt}(G)$$\end{document}Opt(G) without the need to implicitly deduce all zeros of the Sprague-Grundy function. As an example of what such a strategy could be, let us reconsider Chomp (see Section 6.4). When played on a square \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\times k$$\end{document}k×k board, there is an optimal strategy for Chomp that can be described by specifying an action at only \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta (k^2)$$\end{document}Θ(k2) of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta (4^k/\sqrt{k})$$\end{document}Θ(4k/k) game positions. On the first move, take a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(k-1)\times (k-1)$$\end{document}(k-1)×(k-1) bite from the top-right of the board leaving only a L-shaped board with equal length branches for the opponent. On subsequent moves, your opponent will leave you with an L-shaped board with different length branches, in which case you can remove squares from the longer branch to make them equal again. Eventually, your opponent will be left with only the poison square [73].

Obtaining a faster runtime by considering the time to discover these simpler strategies can be formalised due to the fact that Lemma 5.3 was stated more generally than just for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_G$$\end{document}WG. In the remainder of this section we will carry out this analysis for Chomp when played on a square board. For this we will use the following extension of Lemma 5.3, which is tailored to games with an optimal strategy similar to that described for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\times k$$\end{document}k×k Chomp above.

Lemma 7.1

Set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=4000(1+\frac{\log {20}}{\log {3}})$$\end{document}C=4000(1+log20log3). Let G be an n-vertex impartial combinatorial game (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\geqslant 3$$\end{document}n⩾3) with maximum degree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta $$\end{document}Δ. Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1\in F(v_0)$$\end{document}v1∈F(v0) has Grundy value 0, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V'$$\end{document}V′ be the set of vertices that can be reached via a directed path from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1$$\end{document}v1. Set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{s}=\max _{v\in V'}s(v)$$\end{document}s^=maxv∈V′s(v), let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K>0$$\end{document}K>0 be arbitrary, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}$$\end{document}A be described by Algorithm 1 where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{20n^2}\leqslant \gamma \leqslant \frac{1}{20\Delta \max {\{|V'|,3\}}}$$\end{document}120n2⩽γ⩽120Δmax{|V′|,3} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \geqslant C(K+\hat{s}+1)\gamma ^{-(1+2\hat{s})}\log {n}$$\end{document}μ⩾C(K+s^+1)γ-(1+2s^)logn. Then for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \geqslant 1$$\end{document}ℓ⩾1,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}\Biggl [T_\mathcal {A}^G(\text {Opt}(G))\geqslant \mu \biggl (\ell +C\sum _{v\in W_G\cap V'}\gamma ^{-s(v)}\log {(1/\gamma )}\biggr )\Biggr ]\leqslant \ell n^{-K}+e^{-\gamma \ell \mu /2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TAG(Opt(G))⩾μ(ℓ+C∑v∈WG∩V′γ-s(v)log(1/γ))]⩽ℓn-K+e-γℓμ/2.

Proof

We first establish the following claim (where we recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_0$$\end{document}V0 is the set of vertices of G with Grundy value 0).

Claim 7.2

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}x∈XG satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x(v_0)=v_1$$\end{document}x(v0)=v1 and also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x(v)\in V_0$$\end{document}x(v)∈V0 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in W_G\cap V'$$\end{document}v∈WG∩V′, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \text {{Opt}}(G)$$\end{document}x∈Opt(G)

Proof of Claim 7.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1,\ldots ,u_n$$\end{document}u1,…,un be a reverse topological ordering for G which has all vertices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V'$$\end{document}V′ appearing before all vertices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V\setminus V'$$\end{document}V\V′. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in [n]$$\end{document}i∈[n] be such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1=u_i$$\end{document}v1=ui so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V'=\{u_1,\ldots ,u_i\}$$\end{document}V′={u1,…,ui}. Let h be the Sprague-Grundy function for G. By the assumption in the claim, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(x(v))=0$$\end{document}h(x(v))=0 for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in W_G\cap \{u_1,\ldots ,u_i\}$$\end{document}v∈WG∩{u1,…,ui}. Therefore, by swapping the roles of x and y in A2 (Lemma 2.2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_G^{v_1}(y,x)=-1$$\end{document}fG1(y,x)=-1 holds for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}y∈XG. Therefore, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in \mathcal {X}_G$$\end{document}y∈XG we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_G(x,y)=f_G^{v_0}(x,y)=-f_G^{x(v_0)}(y,x)=-f_G^{v_1}(y,x)=1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fG(x,y)=fG0(x,y)=-fGx(v0)(y,x)=-fG1(y,x)=1,and hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \text {{Opt}}(G)$$\end{document}x∈Opt(G). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U=W_G\cap V'$$\end{document}U=WG∩V′ and define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=\{p\in \mathcal {Q}:p(v,V_1)\leqslant \frac{1}{10|U|}\text { for all } v\in U\}$$\end{document}A={p∈Q:p(v,V1)⩽110|U|for allv∈U}. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in A$$\end{document}p∈A and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\sim \text {{Univ}}(\mathcal {X}_G,p)$$\end{document}x∼Univ(XG,p), then Claim 7.2 implies that45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}(x\in \text {{Opt}}(G))\geqslant \mathbb {P}(\wedge _{v\in U}(x(v)\in V_0))\cdot \mathbb {P}(x(v_0)=v_1)\geqslant (1-\textstyle {\frac{1}{10|U|}})^{|U|}\gamma \geqslant \textstyle {\frac{9}{10}}\gamma . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(x∈Opt(G))⩾P(∧v∈U(x(v)∈V0))·P(x(v0)=v1)⩾(1-110|U|)|U|γ⩾910γ.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau =\lfloor 30\sum _{v\in U}\gamma ^{-s(v)}\log {(1/\gamma )}\rfloor $$\end{document}τ=⌊30∑v∈Uγ-s(v)log(1/γ)⌋ so that Lemma 5.3 yields46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}(p_\tau ,p_{\tau +1},\ldots ,p_{\tau +\ell -1}\in A)\geqslant 1-\ell n^{-K}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(pτ,pτ+1,…,pτ+ℓ-1∈A)⩾1-ℓn-K.(See proof of Theorem 5.2 for a full treatment.) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1$$\end{document}E1 be the event that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\notin \text {{Opt}}(G)$$\end{document}x∉Opt(G) occurs in each of the first \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu \ell $$\end{document}μℓ times Line 8 of Algorithm 1 is executed with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_t\in A$$\end{document}pt∈A. We can bound47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}(E_1)\overset{(45)}{\leqslant }(1-\textstyle {\frac{9}{10}}\gamma )^{\mu \ell }\leqslant (1-\textstyle {\frac{1}{2}}\gamma )^{\mu \ell }\leqslant e^{-\gamma \ell \mu /2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(E1)⩽(45)(1-910γ)μℓ⩽(1-12γ)μℓ⩽e-γℓμ/2.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_2$$\end{document}E2 be the event that there are fewer than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell $$\end{document}ℓ values of t where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\leqslant t <\tau +\ell $$\end{document}0⩽t<τ+ℓ for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_t\in A$$\end{document}pt∈A. We can bound48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}(E_2)\overset{(46)}{\leqslant }\ell n^{-K}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(E2)⩽(46)ℓn-K.If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_\mathcal {A}^G(\text {{Opt}}(G))\geqslant \mu \bigl (\ell +C\sum _{v\in W_G\cap V'}\gamma ^{-s(v)}\log {(1/\gamma )}\bigr )$$\end{document}TAG(Opt(G))⩾μ(ℓ+C∑v∈WG∩V′γ-s(v)log(1/γ)) occurs then at least one of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1$$\end{document}E1 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_2$$\end{document}E2 must also occur. Hence, using a union bound,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}\Biggl [T_\mathcal {A}(\text {{Opt}}(G))\geqslant \mu \biggl (\ell +C\sum _{v\in W_G\cap V'}\gamma ^{-s(v)}\log {(1/\gamma )}\biggr )\Biggr ]&\leqslant \mathbb {P}(E_1)+\mathbb {P}(E_2)\\&\hspace{-0.4cm}\overset{(47),(48)}{\leqslant }\ \ell n^{-K}+e^{-\gamma \ell \mu /2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TA(Opt(G))⩾μ(ℓ+C∑v∈WG∩V′γ-s(v)log(1/γ))]⩽P(E1)+P(E2)⩽(47),(48)ℓn-K+e-γℓμ/2,as required. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We are now ready to establish a faster runtime for square Chomp, where as in Section 6 we use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}$$\end{document}A to represent Algorithm 1. The result proves two runtime bounds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Chomp}_{k,k}$$\end{document}CHOMPk,k. The first shows that with essentially the same parameter settings for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ as that implied by Theorem 5.2, the closer analysis here improves the quasipolynomial bound of Proposition 6.4 to a polynomial bound. The second shows that with improved parameter settings for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ, this polynomial bound can be improved further to a logarithmic bound. There is some question about the utility of the second bound when the corresponding parameter settings are determined using a priori knowledge of the optimal strategy for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Chomp}_{k,k}$$\end{document}CHOMPk,k (and so obtaining similar parameter settings in general is impractical), however in application practical settings for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ can be sought using parameter tuning [77], and so the bound is still of interest. While the degrees of the polynomials in both bounds are large, we do not claim them to be close to best possible, and in any case both bounds demonstrate strong asymptotic improvement over Proposition 6.4 is possible. For ease of notation, we prove the tail bounds hold with probability \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n^{-1}$$\end{document}n-1, however the same results can be easily proven for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n^{-K}$$\end{document}n-K with an appropriate rescaling of population size (as with the results of Section 6).

Proposition 7.3

There exist constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C',C''>0$$\end{document}C′,C′′>0 such that the following holds. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =1/(20\Delta n)$$\end{document}γ=1/(20Δn) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =6C(20\Delta n)^7\log {n}$$\end{document}μ=6C(20Δn)7logn (where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta =k^2-1$$\end{document}Δ=k2-1),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}[T_\mathcal {A}(\text {{Opt}}(\textsc {Chomp} _{k,k}))\geqslant C'n^{15}]\leqslant n^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TA(Opt(CHOMPk,k))⩾C′n15]⩽n-1.If instead Algorithm 1 is run using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =1/(20k^4)$$\end{document}γ=1/(20k4) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =6C(20k^4)^7\log {n}$$\end{document}μ=6C(20k4)7logn, then in fact,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}[T_\mathcal {A}(\text {{Opt}}(\textsc {Chomp} _{k,k}))\geqslant C''\log ^{44}{n}]\leqslant n^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TA(Opt(CHOMPk,k))⩾C′′log44n]⩽n-1.

Proof

Recall from Section 2.1 that if the current position for a game is v then the player making the next move has a winning strategy if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(v)\ne 0$$\end{document}h(v)≠0, where h denotes the Sprague-Grundy function. Therefore, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1\in F(v_0)$$\end{document}v1∈F(v0) denotes the position for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Chomp}_{k,k}$$\end{document}CHOMPk,k that has the top-right \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(k-1)\times (k-1)$$\end{document}(k-1)×(k-1) squares removed, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1$$\end{document}v1 has Grundy value 0. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V'$$\end{document}V′ be the set of vertices that can be reached via a directed path from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1$$\end{document}v1 and note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|V'|\leqslant k^2=O(\log ^2{n})$$\end{document}|V′|⩽k2=O(log2n). Next, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V$$\end{document}v∈V, then v can be reached from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_0$$\end{document}v0 in at most 3 moves (first take the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(k-1)\times (k-1)$$\end{document}(k-1)×(k-1) bite, then take one bite from each of the horizontal and vertical branches of the remaining L-shaped board as necessary). Therefore, using Proposition 4.4, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s(v)\leqslant 3$$\end{document}s(v)⩽3 holds for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V'$$\end{document}v∈V′, and hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\max _{v\in V'}s(v)\leqslant 3$$\end{document}maxv∈V′s(v)⩽3. Note that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsc {Chomp}_{k,k}$$\end{document}CHOMPk,k we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta =k^2-1=O(\log ^2{n})$$\end{document}Δ=k2-1=O(log2n).

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=4000(1+\frac{\log {20}}{\log {3}})$$\end{document}C=4000(1+log20log3) as in Lemma 7.1, and set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K=2$$\end{document}K=2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell =1$$\end{document}ℓ=1. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =1/(20\Delta n)$$\end{document}γ=1/(20Δn) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =6C(20\Delta n)^{7}\log {n}=\Theta (n^7\log ^{15}{n})$$\end{document}μ=6C(20Δn)7logn=Θ(n7log15n), then there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C'$$\end{document}C′ such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C'n^{15}\geqslant \mu (1+Ck^2(20k^2n)^{3}\log {(20k^2n)})\geqslant \mu \biggl (\ell +C\sum _{v\in W_G\cap V'}\gamma ^{-s(v)}\log {(1/\gamma )}\biggr ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C′n15⩾μ(1+Ck2(20k2n)3log(20k2n))⩾μ(ℓ+C∑v∈WG∩V′γ-s(v)log(1/γ)).Therefore, by Lemma 7.1 we can deduce that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}[T_\mathcal {A}(\text {{Opt}}(\textsc {Chomp} _{k,k}))\geqslant C'n^{15}]\leqslant \ell n^{-K}+e^{-\gamma \ell \mu /2}\leqslant n^{-2}+e^{-n^6}\leqslant n^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TA(Opt(CHOMPk,k))⩾C′n15]⩽ℓn-K+e-γℓμ/2⩽n-2+e-n6⩽n-1.Next, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =1/(20k^4)$$\end{document}γ=1/(20k4) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =6C(20k^4)^7\log {n}=\Theta (\log ^{29}{n})$$\end{document}μ=6C(20k4)7logn=Θ(log29n), then there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C''$$\end{document}C′′ such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C''\log ^{44}{n}\geqslant \mu (1+Ck^2(20k^4)^3\log {(20k^4)})\geqslant \mu \biggl (\ell +C\sum _{v\in W_G\cap V'}\gamma ^{-s(v)}\log {(1/\gamma )}\biggr ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C′′log44n⩾μ(1+Ck2(20k4)3log(20k4))⩾μ(ℓ+C∑v∈WG∩V′γ-s(v)log(1/γ)).Therefore, by Lemma 7.1 we can deduce that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {P}[T_\mathcal {A}(\text {{Opt}}(\textsc {Chomp} _{k,k}))\geqslant C''\log ^{44}{n}]\leqslant n^{-2}+e^{-2\log {n}}\leqslant n^{-1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P[TA(Opt(CHOMPk,k))⩾C′′log44n]⩽n-2+e-2logn⩽n-1,as required \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Concluding Remarks and Future work

We have proven a general upper bound for the runtime of a coevolutionary instance of UMDA when searching for optimal strategies for combinatorial games. The bound is expressed in terms of switchability, a graph property arising from game play. Because Proposition 4.4 shows that switchability can always be bounded using the diameter of a graph, the upper bound is easily applicable to arbitrary combinatorial games. Using the general bound, we were able to derive polynomial and quasipolynomial runtime bounds for UMDA on several established combinatorial games. We have also considered cases in which the general bound is least likely to offer a tight estimate of runtime, and demonstrated that it is possible to adapt our framework to establish a stronger bounds for specific games.

These results constitute the first rigorous analysis of competitive CoEAs on any turn-based game, and are an important step towards a comprehensive theoretical understanding of game playing using evolutionary techniques. However, as the analysed strategic representations do not scale to games with exponentially many positions, current practical implications are limited. Agents that play large combinatorial games (such as Chess or Go) typically rely on sophisticated models (such as neural networks, genetic programs, decision trees, or game-specific models) that map positions onto policies, and an important topic of future work is to consider whether theoretical analysis of CoEAs on large combinatorial games is possible when using simple instances of such models. A related consideration is the role played by search in combinatorial games. Cutting edge game-playing algorithms such as AlphaZero [8] all rely to some extent on searching possible continuations of the game from a given position when determining a next move, yet such searches play no part in the analysis of this paper. A key open question is whether representations that incorporate search are provably superior to those without when evolved using CoEAs.

As well as more realistic representations, future work should also extend analysis towards broader classes of CoEAs, as the current work only applies to UMDA with binary tournament selection. Algorithm 1 is an example of a single population CoEA, however many CoEAs typically evolve two populations, each representing a different player [9]. Use of a single population CoEA is intuitive for impartial combinatorial games, as the game is essentially symmetric (so what constitutes strong play for one player is also strong for the other). Future work could aim to confirm theoretically whether this intuition is justified, and whether the reverse recommendation should be made if the combinatorial game is not impartial. Determining when to use one population or a two population CoEAs is a critical open question for all applications of CoEAs, and one where theoretical analysis could provide rich insight. Finally, while our results for UMDA contribute to the analysis of EDAs with multi-valued decision variables, future work could also consider the extent to which mutation-based CoEAs can be analysed on combinatorial games.
